I step on the scale in the bathroom every now and then, just out of habit, and the number hasn't budged. It's just steady. So, no, no weight loss and no eating less. My appetite is totally normal, despite everything else being so out of whack.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself feeling worthless or guilty about things more often than not, even when it doesn't seem to make sense?\n\nPatient: I mean... I don't know if 'guilty' is the exact right word, but worthless? Yeah, maybe. It’s more like this constant feeling of just... not being good enough, I guess. \n\nI look around at work, and I keep telling myself that other guys would have adjusted by now, you know? Like, the other guys who started when I did, or even after me, they get these weird schedule changes and they just roll with it. They adjust, they take on more responsibilities, they move up to shift supervisor or they transfer over to the maintenance crew where they actually get to fix the HVAC units and make better money. They figure it out. \n\nAnd then there's me. I’m still just... I'm still just trying not to miss small things at work. Like, I'll catch myself having to double-check if I actually refilled the paper towel dispensers in the second-floor bathrooms, or if I remembered to wipe down the handles on the main entrance doors. Just basic, everyday stuff that should be completely automatic by now, but I have to put all this effort into just making sure I don't screw it up. \n\nAnd because I'm struggling just to keep my head above water with the bare minimum, it really makes me feel like... well, like maybe I’m just not cut out for more than this. Like pushing that cleaning cart and emptying those bins is the absolute best I'm ever going to do in my life. My wife is always telling me I'm a hard worker, and I appreciate that, I really do, but it's hard not to feel pretty low about yourself when you see everyone else moving forward and you're just stuck, worrying about whether you forgot to empty the shredder bin in the accounting office. It just makes you feel really small, you know? Like you're just fundamentally not built for anything better.\n\nDoctor: Even though you still care about the things you used to enjoy, do you feel like you don’t get as much pleasure out of them as you used to?\n\nPatient: I don't think so, no. I mean, I still get the same kick out of things, when I actually have the time and energy to do them, anyway. Like, take fishing, for example. I've always loved fishing. There's this spot out by the reservoir, about a forty-five-minute drive from the house, right where the old county road dead-ends. It's quiet, surrounded by these big, old pine trees, and on a good day, you can pull some really decent bass out of there. I've been going there since I was in my twenties, back when I was still taking a few classes at the community college and thought I was going to get a business degree. \n\nAnyway, I still love going out there. When I'm standing on the bank, casting my line out, watching the bobber just sort of drift on the water... I still get that same feeling of peace, you know? That same little thrill when I feel a tug on the line. It's just as good as it always was. The problem isn't that I don't enjoy it anymore, it's just that with this crazy schedule—working overnight, sleeping all day, trying to squeeze in dinner with my wife before I have to start getting ready for work again—I just don't get out there as much. \n\nOr like watching baseball. I'm a huge fan, have been my whole life. I still have all my old baseball cards in binders in the hall closet. I'm still yelling at the umpire for a bad call, still cheering when my team hits a double in the bottom of the ninth. The excitement is totally still there. I still love the crack of the bat and all the stats. I just... ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, I would. It’s pretty much exactly like that. Most days... honestly, almost every single day, I feel like there’s this heavy, flat feeling just following me around. It’s hard to explain to people who haven't felt it, because it’s not like I’m walking around sobbing or anything dramatic like you see on TV. It’s just this... this weight. Like I’m wearing a lead vest under my uniform or something. 

And it doesn't matter where I am or what I'm doing, it's just always there. I could be at work, you know? It'll be 2:00 AM, and I'm just pushing my cleaning cart down one of those long, empty hallways on the third floor—the ones with that really ugly beige carpet that always smells faintly of industrial floor wax and stale coffee—and it's just me and the hum of the HVAC system. And that heavy feeling is right there, just pressing down on me. I'll be emptying a recycling bin or wiping down a water fountain, and it just feels like everything is muted. 

And it's not just at the building, either. That’s the part that really gets to me, because it follows me home. I’ll be sitting there at the kitchen table with my family, having dinner before I have to go sleep. My wife will be telling me about her day, maybe talking about her sister or something that happened at the grocery store, and I’m sitting there, eating my food, trying so hard to just act normal. I'll nod and smile and try to be present, but inside, I'm just totally flat. It's like there's a thick pane of glass between me and the rest of the room. So, yeah, it's there most of the day, pretty much every day. I keep waiting for it to sort of burn off, like morning fog, but it just sticks around, just this constant, heavy grayness that never really leaves me alone.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n10\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF33.8, F34.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, hi, yeah, thanks for seeing me. I guess I'm here because... well, it's my head. It just doesn't stay on task anymore, and it's starting to really mess with my day-to-day. Like, I work as a building cleaner, right? And I've been trying to move up, studying for this supervisor certification because, you know, I did some college back in the day and I know I can do more, but lately... I don't know. I sit down with the training materials and I'm just reading the exact same line over and over and over again. It's like the words just slide right off my brain and nothing sticks. \n\nAnd it's not just the studying, it's at work too, which is what really pushed me to make the appointment. I'll be right in the middle of doing the supply inventory, which I've done a hundred times, or I'll walk all the way down to the basement to the main supply closet for, say, paper towels or a specific floor buffer pad, and I get there and I just stand there staring at the shelves. I completely forget what I went in there for. It's so frustrating because I know I'm not stupid, but I can't hold onto a single thought for more than a minute. I just end up wasting so much time backtracking and trying to figure out what I was supposed to be doing in the first place, and it's just... it's exhausting trying to force my brain to work like it used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down, hopeless, or losing interest in things you used to enjoy lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean... down is one way to put it, I guess. It's more like... well, it's just this constant thing. Most days, I just feel like there’s this incredibly heavy, just completely flat feeling that’s constantly following me around. Like a shadow, you know? It doesn't even matter what I'm doing. Even when I’m just at work, pushing my cleaning cart down one of those long, empty hallways on the third floor—which are usually so quiet, you know, because everyone’s gone home for the day and it’s just me and the hum of the fluorescent lights. You’d think I’d enjoy the peace and quiet, maybe listen to the radio or something like I used to, but instead it’s just this weight pressing down on me, making every step feel like a chore. \n\nAnd it doesn't stay at work, either, which is the worst part. I bring it home with me. I’ll be sitting there in the living room with my family, and my wife might be talking about her day, or we're eating dinner, and I'm just... sitting there. I try so, so hard to act normal, to smile at the right times and nod and be a good dad and husband, because I really don't want to bring them down with me. But inside, I just feel so completely numb and flat. It takes so much energy just to pretend I’m engaged, to put on that mask, and it’s like... I don't even know why I feel this way, because I love them, I really do, but this heavy feeling just doesn't care. It’s just always there, dragging behind me, making everything feel like wading through wet cement. I just can't seem to shake it off, no matter where I am or what I'm doing, whether I'm mopping a floor or sitting on my own couch... it just doesn't lift.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you don't enjoy things as much as you used to, even activities that you once found pleasurable or rewarding?\n\nPatient: I mean... no, I don't think so. It's not really like that. I wouldn't say I've lost the enjoyment for things, you know? Like, take my weekends, for example. I still really look forward to Sunday afternoons. Usually, if the weather's decent, I'll take the kids down to that little park over by the old water tower—the one with the big wooden playground structure that they just renovated last year. And I still really get a kick out of watching them run around. When my youngest does this little goofy dance she does on the swings, I still laugh, I still feel that happiness, you know? It's not like the joy is just completely gone or anything. \n\nOr like, with my wife, we have this tradition where we make a huge bowl of popcorn on Friday nights and watch a movie, usually some old sci-fi thing because I'm a huge nerd for that stuff, have been ever since I was taking a couple of astronomy electives back in community college. I've actually got this whole shelf of paperbacks out in the garage that I've been collecting for years. And I still love those books, I still want to read them—even if, like I was saying earlier, my brain won't let me focus on the words right now. But the actual feeling of liking them? That hasn't changed. \n\nI still care about my hobbies, I still want to do them, and I still like them when I do. It's just that I have to drag that heavy, flat feeling along with me while I'm doing them, which makes it all harder. But the actual things I love? No, I wouldn't say I don't enjoy them anymore. The enjoyment itself is definitely still there underneath it all, if that makes any sense.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way? Has this heavy, flat feeling been present most days, and has it lasted for most of the day, for weeks or even months?\n\nPatient: Weeks or months? Oh, man, I wish it was just a few weeks. No, it’s... it’s been a lot longer than that. It’s been years. About three years, actually, if I really sit down and trace it back. Yeah, three years. \n\nI know exactly when it started, too, because it was right around the time the management company brought in this new contractor for our cleaning services. Before that, I had a pretty steady routine, you know? I knew where I was going to be and when. But this new contractor, they came in and just completely overhauled how everything worked to cut costs or whatever. They decided to change us to these rotating evening and overnight shifts, and they moved us to the larger properties—these massive, sprawling corporate buildings downtown with, like, endless floors of cubicles and glass that take forever to get through. \n\nSo one week I’d be working, say, 4:00 PM to midnight, and then the very next week they’d flip me to the graveyard shift, like 11:00 PM to 7:00 AM, just completely destroying my internal clock. And you’d think, you know, maybe you just get tired from the schedule, but it wasn't just being physically tired. That was when this... this heavy, flat feeling really started creeping in and settling over me. I remember walking those huge, empty floors in the middle of the night, buffing the marble in those giant lobbies all by myself, and just feeling this incredible weight start to press down on me out of nowhere. \n\nAnd the thing is, it just never lifted. Even when I'd get a couple of days off, or when my body supposedly adjusted to the nights, the feeling stayed. So yeah, for about three years now, it’s been there most days. Honestly, almost every single day, for the entire day. I wake up and it’s already there waiting for me, and it stays with me through the whole shift, through the drive home in the morning traffic, through dinner with my wife... it’s just been this constant, exhausting shadow ever since that contractor messed with our schedules three years ago. It just hasn't stopped.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt a loss of energy or noticed that even small tasks feel physically exhausting, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I mean... no, not exactly. It's not like I don't have the physical energy to do things, you know? Like, I wouldn't say I'm physically exhausted or that my muscles are giving out on me or anything like that. I can still do my job. I still push that heavy cleaning cart around, I still lift the big trash bags out of those industrial bins—and let me tell you, some of those bins get incredibly heavy, especially in the breakrooms where people just dump half-eaten lunches and coffee cups all day long. I can still haul all that down to the loading dock without feeling like I need to collapse. \n\nAnd it's the same at home. If the lawn needs mowing, I still get out there and push the mower around the yard, even in the dead of summer when the humidity is just awful and you're sweating through your shirt before you even finish the front yard. I still have the physical stamina to do all of that. So it's not a lack of physical energy, and small tasks don't feel physically exhausting to me. \n\nIt's more like... like I was saying before, it's that heavy, flat feeling that makes everything *feel* like a chore, mentally. It’s like, the physical energy is there, my body works fine, but mentally, I have to drag myself through the motions because of that shadow hanging over me. But no, as far as actual physical energy or feeling physically exhausted by small tasks? No, I haven't noticed that. I'm still perfectly capable of doing all the physical stuff I need to do, day in and day out.\n\nDoctor: Have you been sleeping more than usual lately, like needing a lot more sleep or napping during the day, almost every day?\n\nPatient: Sleeping more? No, definitely not sleeping more. I mean, I wish I could just sleep the day away sometimes, but that's not what happens. My sleep has just been completely messed up for a really long time now. It goes back to those schedule changes I was telling you about earlier. \n\nLike, after an overnight shift—say I get off at 7:00 AM, right? I drive home, the sun is coming up, traffic is starting to get heavy with all the regular nine-to-five people commuting in, and by the time I get home, take off my boots, and crawl into bed, my body is just... confused. I'll have the blackout curtains drawn tight in the bedroom, the box fan going on high for white noise because the neighborhood is just waking up and there's always delivery trucks or people mowing their lawns, but I’ll just lie there. I am so tired from being on my feet all night, but I'm just wired. It's this awful feeling where my eyes are heavy, but my mind is just buzzing and won't shut off. I'll just stare at the ceiling, tired but completely wired. \n\nAnd then, the kicker is, even when I do finally drift off, and even if I manage to stay asleep for a decent number of hours—like, say I actually get a solid seven or eight hours without the dog barking or the doorbell ringing—when I finally wake up in the late afternoon, I don't feel rested at all. I wake up feeling like I barely rested, like I just closed my eyes for five minutes. It's incredibly frustrating because you'd think after working all night you'd just pass out and wake up recharged, but my sleep is just totally broken. So no, I'm not sleeping more or taking extra daytime naps, I'm just stuck in this cycle where the sleep I do get doesn't actually do anything for me.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your appetite or weight, like eating much less than usual or losing weight without trying?\n\nPatient: No, no changes like that. My appetite is... well, it's pretty much the same as it's always been. I definitely haven't been eating less or losing any weight. If anything, I'm probably eating the exact same things I've eaten for years. \n\nYou know, when you work those weird shifts, especially the overnights, your eating schedule gets just as messed up as your sleep. So what happens is, you end up relying on whatever is open at 3:00 AM or whatever you can quickly heat up in the microwave in the breakroom. I usually pack a lunch—well, I guess you'd call it \"lunch,\" even though I eat it at like 2:30 in the morning. My wife makes these great turkey and swiss sandwiches, and she always packs a little bag of chips and an apple. And I eat it all, every single night, sitting in that quiet, echoing breakroom under those harsh fluorescent lights. I never leave anything behind. \n\nAnd then when I get home in the morning, around 8:00 AM, my wife is usually having breakfast, so I'll sit down and have a bowl of cereal or some toast with her before I try to go to sleep. And then when I wake up in the late afternoon, we have dinner together. We do a lot of chicken, rice, maybe some pasta—just regular, filling meals. And I always finish my plate. Sometimes I even go back for seconds if she makes that baked ziti I really like. \n\nSo, yeah, I'm definitely not skipping meals or losing my appetite. My clothes all fit the exact same way they did three years ago before all this schedule nonsense started. I haven't had to tighten my belt a notch or anything like that. I step on the scale in the bathroom every now and then, just out of habit, and the number hasn't budged. It's just steady. So, no, no weight loss and no eating less. My appetite is totally normal, despite everything else being so out of whack.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself feeling worthless or guilty about things more often than not, even when it doesn't seem to make sense?\n\nPatient: I mean... I don't know if 'guilty' is the exact right word, but worthless? Yeah, maybe. It’s more like this constant feeling of just... not being good enough, I guess. \n\nI look around at work, and I keep telling myself that other guys would have adjusted by now, you know? Like, the other guys who started when I did, or even after me, they get these weird schedule changes and they just roll with it. They adjust, they take on more responsibilities, they move up to shift supervisor or they transfer over to the maintenance crew where they actually get to fix the HVAC units and make better money. They figure it out. \n\nAnd then there's me. I’m still just... I'm still just trying not to miss small things at work. Like, I'll catch myself having to double-check if I actually refilled the paper towel dispensers in the second-floor bathrooms, or if I remembered to wipe down the handles on the main entrance doors. Just basic, everyday stuff that should be completely automatic by now, but I have to put all this effort into just making sure I don't screw it up. \n\nAnd because I'm struggling just to keep my head above water with the bare minimum, it really makes me feel like... well, like maybe I’m just not cut out for more than this. Like pushing that cleaning cart and emptying those bins is the absolute best I'm ever going to do in my life. My wife is always telling me I'm a hard worker, and I appreciate that, I really do, but it's hard not to feel pretty low about yourself when you see everyone else moving forward and you're just stuck, worrying about whether you forgot to empty the shredder bin in the accounting office. It just makes you feel really small, you know? Like you're just fundamentally not built for anything better.\n\nDoctor: Even though you still care about the things you used to enjoy, do you feel like you don’t get as much pleasure out of them as you used to?\n\nPatient: I don't think so, no. I mean, I still get the same kick out of things, when I actually have the time and energy to do them, anyway. Like, take fishing, for example. I've always loved fishing. There's this spot out by the reservoir, about a forty-five-minute drive from the house, right where the old county road dead-ends. It's quiet, surrounded by these big, old pine trees, and on a good day, you can pull some really decent bass out of there. I've been going there since I was in my twenties, back when I was still taking a few classes at the community college and thought I was going to get a business degree. \n\nAnyway, I still love going out there. When I'm standing on the bank, casting my line out, watching the bobber just sort of drift on the water... I still get that same feeling of peace, you know? That same little thrill when I feel a tug on the line. It's just as good as it always was. The problem isn't that I don't enjoy it anymore, it's just that with this crazy schedule—working overnight, sleeping all day, trying to squeeze in dinner with my wife before I have to start getting ready for work again—I just don't get out there as much. \n\nOr like watching baseball. I'm a huge fan, have been my whole life. I still have all my old baseball cards in binders in the hall closet. When I actually manage to sit down on the couch on my day off—if I'm not completely zombified from the shift work—and catch a game on TV, I'm still right there in it. I'm still yelling at the umpire for a bad call, still cheering when my team hits a double in the bottom of the ninth. The excitement is totally still there. I still love the crack of the bat and all the stats. I just... I might fall asleep during the seventh-inning stretch because I've been up for twenty hours, but the actual enjoyment of the game hasn't changed a bit. So, yeah, the pleasure is definitely still there for the things I like, it's just the logistics of actually doing them that's the nightmare right now.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your mood has been persistently low, feeling that heavy, flat sensation you described, for most of the day, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I would. It’s pretty much exactly like that. Most days... honestly, almost every single day, I feel like there’s this heavy, flat feeling just following me around. It’s hard to explain to people who haven't felt it, because it’s not like I’m walking around sobbing or anything dramatic like you see on TV. It’s just this... this weight. Like I’m wearing a lead vest under my uniform or something. \n\nAnd it doesn't matter where I am or what I'm doing, it's just always there. I could be at work, you know? It'll be 2:00 AM, and I'm just pushing my cleaning cart down one of those long, empty hallways on the third floor—the ones with that really ugly beige carpet that always smells faintly of industrial floor wax and stale coffee—and it's just me and the hum of the HVAC system. And that heavy feeling is right there, just pressing down on me. I'll be emptying a recycling bin or wiping down a water fountain, and it just feels like everything is muted. \n\nAnd it's not just at the building, either. That’s the part that really gets to me, because it follows me home. I’ll be sitting there at the kitchen table with my family, having dinner before I have to go sleep. My wife will be telling me about her day, maybe talking about her sister or something that happened at the grocery store, and I’m sitting there, eating my food, trying so hard to just act normal. I'll nod and smile and try to be present, but inside, I'm just totally flat. It's like there's a thick pane of glass between me and the rest of the room. So, yeah, it's there most of the day, pretty much every day. I keep waiting for it to sort of burn off, like morning fog, but it just sticks around, just this constant, heavy grayness that never really leaves me alone.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Yeah, like I was just saying, it really is nearly every day. Most days, I just feel like there’s this heavy, flat feeling following me around everywhere I go, like a shadow I can't shake. It just doesn't stay on task anymore, and it's starting to really mess with my day-to-day. Like, I work as a building cleaner, right? And I've been trying to move up, studying for this supervisor certification because, you know, I did some college back in the day and I know I can do more, but lately... I don't know. I sit down with the training materials and I'm just reading the exact same line over and over and over again. It's like the words just slide right off my brain and nothing sticks. \n\nAnd it's not just the studying, it's at work too, which is what really pushed me to make the appointment. I'll be right in the middle of doing the supply inventory, which I've done a hundred times, or I'll walk all the way down to the basement to the main supply closet for, say, paper towels or a specific floor buffer pad, and I get there and I just stand there staring at the shelves. I completely forget what I went in there for. It's so frustrating because I know I'm not stupid, but I can't hold onto a single thought for more than a minute. I just end up wasting so much time backtracking and trying to figure out what I was supposed to be doing in the first place, and it's just... it's exhausting trying to force my brain to work like it used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down, hopeless, or losing interest in things you used to enjoy lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean... down is one way to put it, I guess. It's more like... well, it's just this constant thing. Most days, I just feel like there’s this incredibly heavy, just completely flat feeling that’s constantly following me around. Like a shadow, you know? It doesn't even matter what I'm doing. Even when I’m just at work, pushing my cleaning cart down one of those long, empty hallways on the third floor—which are usually so quiet, you know, because everyone’s gone home for the day and it’s just me and the hum of the fluorescent lights. You’d think I’d enjoy the peace and quiet, maybe listen to the radio or something like I used to, but instead it’s just this weight pressing down on me, making every step feel like a chore. \n\nAnd it doesn't stay at work, either, which is the worst part. I bring it home with me. I’ll be sitting there in the living room with my family, and my wife might be talking about her day, or we're eating dinner, and I'm just... sitting there. I try so, so hard to act normal, to smile at the right times and nod and be a good dad and husband, because I really don't want to bring them down with me. But inside, I just feel so completely numb and flat. It takes so much energy just to pretend I’m engaged, to put on that mask, and it’s like... I don't even know why I feel this way, because I love them, I really do, but this heavy feeling just doesn't care. It’s just always there, dragging behind me, making everything feel like wading through wet cement. I just can't seem to shake it off, no matter where I am or what I'm doing, whether I'm mopping a floor or sitting on my own couch... it just doesn't lift.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you don't enjoy things as much as you used to, even activities that you once found pleasurable or rewarding?\n\nPatient: I mean... no, I don't think so. It's not really like that. I wouldn't say I've lost the enjoyment for things, you know? Like, take my weekends, for example. I still really look forward to Sunday afternoons. Usually, if the weather's decent, I'll take the kids down to that little park over by the old water tower—the one with the big wooden playground structure that they just renovated last year. And I still really get a kick out of watching them run around. When my youngest does this little goofy dance she does on the swings, I still laugh, I still feel that happiness, you know? It's not like the joy is just completely gone or anything. \n\nOr like, with my wife, we have this tradition where we make a huge bowl of popcorn on Friday nights and watch a movie, usually some old sci-fi thing because I'm a huge nerd for that stuff, have been ever since I was taking a couple of astronomy electives back in community college. I've actually got this whole shelf of paperbacks out in the garage that I've been collecting for years. And I still love those books, I still want to read them—even if, like I was saying earlier, my brain won't let me focus on the words right now. But the actual feeling of liking them? That hasn't changed. \n\nI still care about my hobbies, I still want to do them, and I still like them when I do. It's just that I have to drag that heavy, flat feeling along with me while I'm doing them, which makes it all harder. But the actual things I love? No, I wouldn't say I don't enjoy them anymore. The enjoyment itself is definitely still there underneath it all, if that makes any sense.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way? Has this heavy, flat feeling been present most days, and has it lasted for most of the day, for weeks or even months?\n\nPatient: Weeks or months? Oh, man, I wish it was just a few weeks. No, it’s... it’s been a lot longer than that. It’s been years. About three years, actually, if I really sit down and trace it back. Yeah, three years. \n\nI know exactly when it started, too, because it was right around the time the management company brought in this new contractor for our cleaning services. Before that, I had a pretty steady routine, you know? I knew where I was going to be and when. But this new contractor, they came in and just completely overhauled how everything worked to cut costs or whatever. They decided to change us to these rotating evening and overnight shifts, and they moved us to the larger properties—these massive, sprawling corporate buildings downtown with, like, endless floors of cubicles and glass that take forever to get through. \n\nSo one week I’d be working, say, 4:00 PM to midnight, and then the very next week they’d flip me to the graveyard shift, like 11:00 PM to 7:00 AM, just completely destroying my internal clock. And you’d think, you know, maybe you just get tired from the schedule, but it wasn't just being physically tired. That was when this... this heavy, flat feeling really started creeping in and settling over me. I remember walking those huge, empty floors in the middle of the night, buffing the marble in those giant lobbies all by myself, and just feeling this incredible weight start to press down on me out of nowhere. \n\nAnd the thing is, it just never lifted. Even when I'd get a couple of days off, or when my body supposedly adjusted to the nights, the feeling stayed. So yeah, for about three years now, it’s been there most days. Honestly, almost every single day, for the entire day. I wake up and it’s already there waiting for me, and it stays with me through the whole shift, through the drive home in the morning traffic, through dinner with my wife... it’s just been this constant, exhausting shadow ever since that contractor messed with our schedules three years ago. It just hasn't stopped.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt a loss of energy or noticed that even small tasks feel physically exhausting, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I mean... no, not exactly. It's not like I don't have the physical energy to do things, you know? Like, I wouldn't say I'm physically exhausted or that my muscles are giving out on me or anything like that. I can still do my job. I still push that heavy cleaning cart around, I still lift the big trash bags out of those industrial bins—and let me tell you, some of those bins get incredibly heavy, especially in the breakrooms where people just dump half-eaten lunches and coffee cups all day long. I can still haul all that down to the loading dock without feeling like I need to collapse. \n\nAnd it's the same at home. If the lawn needs mowing, I still get out there and push the mower around the yard, even in the dead of summer when the humidity is just awful and you're sweating through your shirt before you even finish the front yard. I still have the physical stamina to do all of that. So it's not a lack of physical energy, and small tasks don't feel physically exhausting to me. \n\nIt's more like... like I was saying before, it's that heavy, flat feeling that makes everything *feel* like a chore, mentally. It’s like, the physical energy is there, my body works fine, but mentally, I have to drag myself through the motions because of that shadow hanging over me. But no, as far as actual physical energy or feeling physically exhausted by small tasks? No, I haven't noticed that. I'm still perfectly capable of doing all the physical stuff I need to do, day in and day out.\n\nDoctor: Have you been sleeping more than usual lately, like needing a lot more sleep or napping during the day, almost every day?\n\nPatient: Sleeping more? No, definitely not sleeping more. I mean, I wish I could just sleep the day away sometimes, but that's not what happens. My sleep has just been completely messed up for a really long time now. It goes back to those schedule changes I was telling you about earlier. \n\nLike, after an overnight shift—say I get off at 7:00 AM, right? I drive home, the sun is coming up, traffic is starting to get heavy with all the regular nine-to-five people commuting in, and by the time I get home, take off my boots, and crawl into bed, my body is just... confused. I'll have the blackout curtains drawn tight in the bedroom, the box fan going on high for white noise because the neighborhood is just waking up and there's always delivery trucks or people mowing their lawns, but I’ll just lie there. I am so tired from being on my feet all night, but I'm just wired. It's this awful feeling where my eyes are heavy, but my mind is just buzzing and won't shut off. I'll just stare at the ceiling, tired but completely wired. \n\nAnd then, the kicker is, even when I do finally drift off, and even if I manage to stay asleep for a decent number of hours—like, say I actually get a solid seven or eight hours without the dog barking or the doorbell ringing—when I finally wake up in the late afternoon, I don't feel rested at all. I wake up feeling like I barely rested, like I just closed my eyes for five minutes. It's incredibly frustrating because you'd think after working all night you'd just pass out and wake up recharged, but my sleep is just totally broken. So no, I'm not sleeping more or taking extra daytime naps, I'm just stuck in this cycle where the sleep I do get doesn't actually do anything for me.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your appetite or weight, like eating much less than usual or losing weight without trying?\n\nPatient: No, no changes like that. My appetite is... well, it's pretty much the same as it's always been. I definitely haven't been eating less or losing any weight. If anything, I'm probably eating the exact same things I've eaten for years. \n\nYou know, when you work those weird shifts, especially the overnights, your eating schedule gets just as messed up as your sleep. So what happens is, you end up relying on whatever is open at 3:00 AM or whatever you can quickly heat up in the microwave in the breakroom. I usually pack a lunch—well, I guess you'd call it \"lunch,\" even though I eat it at like 2:30 in the morning. My wife makes these great turkey and swiss sandwiches, and she always packs a little bag of chips and an apple. And I eat it all, every single night, sitting in that quiet, echoing breakroom under those harsh fluorescent lights. I never leave anything behind. \n\nAnd then when I get home in the morning, around 8:00 AM, my wife is usually having breakfast, so I'll sit down and have a bowl of cereal or some toast with her before I try to go to sleep. And then when I wake up in the late afternoon, we have dinner together. We do a lot of chicken, rice, maybe some pasta—just regular, filling meals. And I always finish my plate. Sometimes I even go back for seconds if she makes that baked ziti I really like. \n\nSo, yeah, I'm definitely not skipping meals or losing my appetite. My clothes all fit the exact same way they did three years ago before all this schedule nonsense started. I haven't had to tighten my belt a notch or anything like that. I step on the scale in the bathroom every now and then, just out of habit, and the number hasn't budged. It's just steady. So, no, no weight loss and no eating less. My appetite is totally normal, despite everything else being so out of whack.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself feeling worthless or guilty about things more often than not, even when it doesn't seem to make sense?\n\nPatient: I mean... I don't know if 'guilty' is the exact right word, but worthless? Yeah, maybe. It’s more like this constant feeling of just... not being good enough, I guess. \n\nI look around at work, and I keep telling myself that other guys would have adjusted by now, you know? Like, the other guys who started when I did, or even after me, they get these weird schedule changes and they just roll with it. They adjust, they take on more responsibilities, they move up to shift supervisor or they transfer over to the maintenance crew where they actually get to fix the HVAC units and make better money. They figure it out. \n\nAnd then there's me. I’m still just... I'm still just trying not to miss small things at work. Like, I'll catch myself having to double-check if I actually refilled the paper towel dispensers in the second-floor bathrooms, or if I remembered to wipe down the handles on the main entrance doors. Just basic, everyday stuff that should be completely automatic by now, but I have to put all this effort into just making sure I don't screw it up. \n\nAnd because I'm struggling just to keep my head above water with the bare minimum, it really makes me feel like... well, like maybe I’m just not cut out for more than this. Like pushing that cleaning cart and emptying those bins is the absolute best I'm ever going to do in my life. My wife is always telling me I'm a hard worker, and I appreciate that, I really do, but it's hard not to feel pretty low about yourself when you see everyone else moving forward and you're just stuck, worrying about whether you forgot to empty the shredder bin in the accounting office. It just makes you feel really small, you know? Like you're just fundamentally not built for anything better.\n\nDoctor: Even though you still care about the things you used to enjoy, do you feel like you don’t get as much pleasure out of them as you used to?\n\nPatient: I don't think so, no. I mean, I still get the same kick out of things, when I actually have the time and energy to do them, anyway. Like, take fishing, for example. I've always loved fishing. There's this spot out by the reservoir, about a forty-five-minute drive from the house, right where the old county road dead-ends. It's quiet, surrounded by these big, old pine trees, and on a good day, you can pull some really decent bass out of there. I've been going there since I was in my twenties, back when I was still taking a few classes at the community college and thought I was going to get a business degree. \n\nAnyway, I still love going out there. When I'm standing on the bank, casting my line out, watching the bobber just sort of drift on the water... I still get that same feeling of peace, you know? That same little thrill when I feel a tug on the line. It's just as good as it always was. The problem isn't that I don't enjoy it anymore, it's just that with this crazy schedule—working overnight, sleeping all day, trying to squeeze in dinner with my wife before I have to start getting ready for work again—I just don't get out there as much. \n\nOr like watching baseball. I'm a huge fan, have been my whole life. I still have all my old baseball cards in binders in the hall closet. When I actually manage to sit down on the couch on my day off—if I'm not completely zombified from the shift work—and catch a game on TV, I'm still right there in it. I'm still yelling at the umpire for a bad call, still cheering when my team hits a double in the bottom of the ninth. The excitement is totally still there. I still love the crack of the bat and all the stats. I just... I might fall asleep during the seventh-inning stretch because I've been up for twenty hours, but the actual enjoyment of the game hasn't changed a bit. So, yeah, the pleasure is definitely still there for the things I like, it's just the logistics of actually doing them that's the nightmare right now.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your mood has been persistently low, feeling that heavy, flat sensation you described, for most of the day, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I would. It’s pretty much exactly like that. Most days... honestly, almost every single day, I feel like there’s this heavy, flat feeling just following me around. It’s hard to explain to people who haven't felt it, because it’s not like I’m walking around sobbing or anything dramatic like you see on TV. It’s just this... this weight. Like I’m wearing a lead vest under my uniform or something. \n\nAnd it doesn't matter where I am or what I'm doing, it's just always there. I could be at work, you know? It'll be 2:00 AM, and I'm just pushing my cleaning cart down one of those long, empty hallways on the third floor—the ones with that really ugly beige carpet that always smells faintly of industrial floor wax and stale coffee—and it's just me and the hum of the HVAC system. And that heavy feeling is right there, just pressing down on me. I'll be emptying a recycling bin or wiping down a water fountain, and it just feels like everything is muted. \n\nAnd it's not just at the building, either. That’s the part that really gets to me, because it follows me home. I’ll be sitting there at the kitchen table with my family, having dinner before I have to go sleep. My wife will be telling me about her day, maybe talking about her sister or something that happened at the grocery store, and I’m sitting there, eating my food, trying so hard to just act normal. I'll nod and smile and try to be present, but inside, I'm just totally flat. It's like there's a thick pane of glass between me and the rest of the room. So, yeah, it's there most of the day, pretty much every day. I keep waiting for it to sort of burn off, like morning fog, but it just sticks around, just this constant, heavy grayness that never really leaves me alone.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your mood has been persistently low, feeling that heavy, flat sensation you described, for most of the day, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yeah, like I was just saying, it really is nearly every day. Most days, I just feel like there’s this heavy, flat feeling following me around everywhere I go, like a shadow I can't shake. It doesn't even matter what the setting is or what I'm doing. I could be at work, just pushing my supply cart down an empty hallway at three in the morning—you know, when the building is totally dead and the only sound is the squeak of the cart's front wheel and the hum of the vending machines in the breakroom. I'll just be walking past all these locked office doors, and that weight is right there, pressing down on me. \n\nAnd it's not like it magically goes away when I clock out and go home, either. I think that's the part that exhausts me the most, honestly. I'll be sitting right there in the living room with my family, and my kids will be laughing at something on the TV, or my wife will be telling me about a phone call she had with her mom, and I'm just sitting there, trying so, so hard to just act normal. I'll force myself to smile, and I make sure to nod and say 'oh, really?' at the right times so they don't think anything is wrong, but underneath it all, I just feel completely flat and hollowed out. It takes so much energy just to put on that mask for them. So, yeah, whether I'm completely alone at work or surrounded by my family at home, that heavy feeling is just always there.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Molasses... yeah, that's exactly it. Actually, honestly, that's a big part of why I finally came in to see someone. My head just... it just doesn’t stay on task anymore. It’s like my brain is just constantly stalling out on me. 

Like, at work, I'll be right in the middle of doing the monthly inventory. I have this whole system down because I've been doing it for years, right? I'll be down in the basement storage area, checking the levels on the floor wax and the industrial degreasers, and I'll realize I need to grab a specific clipboard or check a stock number from the main supply closet. The main closet is all the way on the other side of the building, past the boiler room and the old service elevators. So I'll walk all the way over there, my keys jingling, thinking about what I need to do next. But the second I unlock that door and pull the pull-string for the light, I just... I draw a complete blank. I'll just stand there staring at the shelves of paper towels and mop heads, completely forgetting what I even went to the supply closet for. I'll just stand there feeling stupid, trying to retrace my steps in my head, and it’s just empty. 

And it's really messing with my plans outside of the day-to-day cleaning, too. I've been trying to study for the supervisor certification. I took some business classes at the community college back in my twenties, so it's not like I don't know how to study or how to handle a textbook, you know? I know how to put the work in. But lately, I'll sit down at the kitchen table on my night off with the study guide—it's this thick binder with all the OSHA regulations and scheduling protocols and all that—and I just can't process any of it. I will literally sit there and keep reading the exact same line over and over and over. I'll read a sentence about, I don't know, proper ventilation requirements for chemical mixing, and my eyes will go across the words, but nothing actually enters my brain. I'll get to the end of the page and realize I haven't absorbed a single thing. It’s incredibly frustrating because I really want to pass this test and get a better shift, but it just takes me forever to get through even one paragraph now. My thoughts are just so slow and foggy.

Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, hi, yeah, thanks for seeing me. I guess I'm here because... well, it's my head. It just doesn't stay on task anymore, and it's starting to really mess with my day-to-day. Like, I work as a building cleaner, right? And I've been trying to move up, studying for this supervisor certification because, you know, I did some college back in the day and I know I can do more, but lately... I don't know. I sit down with the training materials and I'm just reading the exact same line over and over and over again. It's like the words just slide right off my brain and nothing sticks. \n\nAnd it's not just the studying, it's at work too, which is what really pushed me to make the appointment. I'll be right in the middle of doing the supply inventory, which I've done a hundred times, or I'll walk all the way down to the basement to the main supply closet for, say, paper towels or a specific floor buffer pad, and I get there and I just stand there staring at the shelves. I completely forget what I went in there for. It's so frustrating because I know I'm not stupid, but I can't hold onto a single thought for more than a minute. I just end up wasting so much time backtracking and trying to figure out what I was supposed to be doing in the first place, and it's just... it's exhausting trying to force my brain to work like it used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down, hopeless, or losing interest in things you used to enjoy lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean... down is one way to put it, I guess. It's more like... well, it's just this constant thing. Most days, I just feel like there’s this incredibly heavy, just completely flat feeling that’s constantly following me around. Like a shadow, you know? It doesn't even matter what I'm doing. Even when I’m just at work, pushing my cleaning cart down one of those long, empty hallways on the third floor—which are usually so quiet, you know, because everyone’s gone home for the day and it’s just me and the hum of the fluorescent lights. You’d think I’d enjoy the peace and quiet, maybe listen to the radio or something like I used to, but instead it’s just this weight pressing down on me, making every step feel like a chore. \n\nAnd it doesn't stay at work, either, which is the worst part. I bring it home with me. I’ll be sitting there in the living room with my family, and my wife might be talking about her day, or we're eating dinner, and I'm just... sitting there. I try so, so hard to act normal, to smile at the right times and nod and be a good dad and husband, because I really don't want to bring them down with me. But inside, I just feel so completely numb and flat. It takes so much energy just to pretend I’m engaged, to put on that mask, and it’s like... I don't even know why I feel this way, because I love them, I really do, but this heavy feeling just doesn't care. It’s just always there, dragging behind me, making everything feel like wading through wet cement. I just can't seem to shake it off, no matter where I am or what I'm doing, whether I'm mopping a floor or sitting on my own couch... it just doesn't lift.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you don't enjoy things as much as you used to, even activities that you once found pleasurable or rewarding?\n\nPatient: I mean... no, I don't think so. It's not really like that. I wouldn't say I've lost the enjoyment for things, you know? Like, take my weekends, for example. I still really look forward to Sunday afternoons. Usually, if the weather's decent, I'll take the kids down to that little park over by the old water tower—the one with the big wooden playground structure that they just renovated last year. And I still really get a kick out of watching them run around. When my youngest does this little goofy dance she does on the swings, I still laugh, I still feel that happiness, you know? It's not like the joy is just completely gone or anything. \n\nOr like, with my wife, we have this tradition where we make a huge bowl of popcorn on Friday nights and watch a movie, usually some old sci-fi thing because I'm a huge nerd for that stuff, have been ever since I was taking a couple of astronomy electives back in community college. I've actually got this whole shelf of paperbacks out in the garage that I've been collecting for years. And I still love those books, I still want to read them—even if, like I was saying earlier, my brain won't let me focus on the words right now. But the actual feeling of liking them? That hasn't changed. \n\nI still care about my hobbies, I still want to do them, and I still like them when I do. It's just that I have to drag that heavy, flat feeling along with me while I'm doing them, which makes it all harder. But the actual things I love? No, I wouldn't say I don't enjoy them anymore. The enjoyment itself is definitely still there underneath it all, if that makes any sense.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way? Has this heavy, flat feeling been present most days, and has it lasted for most of the day, for weeks or even months?\n\nPatient: Weeks or months? Oh, man, I wish it was just a few weeks. No, it’s... it’s been a lot longer than that. It’s been years. About three years, actually, if I really sit down and trace it back. Yeah, three years. \n\nI know exactly when it started, too, because it was right around the time the management company brought in this new contractor for our cleaning services. Before that, I had a pretty steady routine, you know? I knew where I was going to be and when. But this new contractor, they came in and just completely overhauled how everything worked to cut costs or whatever. They decided to change us to these rotating evening and overnight shifts, and they moved us to the larger properties—these massive, sprawling corporate buildings downtown with, like, endless floors of cubicles and glass that take forever to get through. \n\nSo one week I’d be working, say, 4:00 PM to midnight, and then the very next week they’d flip me to the graveyard shift, like 11:00 PM to 7:00 AM, just completely destroying my internal clock. And you’d think, you know, maybe you just get tired from the schedule, but it wasn't just being physically tired. That was when this... this heavy, flat feeling really started creeping in and settling over me. I remember walking those huge, empty floors in the middle of the night, buffing the marble in those giant lobbies all by myself, and just feeling this incredible weight start to press down on me out of nowhere. \n\nAnd the thing is, it just never lifted. Even when I'd get a couple of days off, or when my body supposedly adjusted to the nights, the feeling stayed. So yeah, for about three years now, it’s been there most days. Honestly, almost every single day, for the entire day. I wake up and it’s already there waiting for me, and it stays with me through the whole shift, through the drive home in the morning traffic, through dinner with my wife... it’s just been this constant, exhausting shadow ever since that contractor messed with our schedules three years ago. It just hasn't stopped.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt a loss of energy or noticed that even small tasks feel physically exhausting, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I mean... no, not exactly. It's not like I don't have the physical energy to do things, you know? Like, I wouldn't say I'm physically exhausted or that my muscles are giving out on me or anything like that. I can still do my job. I still push that heavy cleaning cart around, I still lift the big trash bags out of those industrial bins—and let me tell you, some of those bins get incredibly heavy, especially in the breakrooms where people just dump half-eaten lunches and coffee cups all day long. I can still haul all that down to the loading dock without feeling like I need to collapse. \n\nAnd it's the same at home. If the lawn needs mowing, I still get out there and push the mower around the yard, even in the dead of summer when the humidity is just awful and you're sweating through your shirt before you even finish the front yard. I still have the physical stamina to do all of that. So it's not a lack of physical energy, and small tasks don't feel physically exhausting to me. \n\nIt's more like... like I was saying before, it's that heavy, flat feeling that makes everything *feel* like a chore, mentally. It’s like, the physical energy is there, my body works fine, but mentally, I have to drag myself through the motions because of that shadow hanging over me. But no, as far as actual physical energy or feeling physically exhausted by small tasks? No, I haven't noticed that. I'm still perfectly capable of doing all the physical stuff I need to do, day in and day out.\n\nDoctor: Have you been sleeping more than usual lately, like needing a lot more sleep or napping during the day, almost every day?\n\nPatient: Sleeping more? No, definitely not sleeping more. I mean, I wish I could just sleep the day away sometimes, but that's not what happens. My sleep has just been completely messed up for a really long time now. It goes back to those schedule changes I was telling you about earlier. \n\nLike, after an overnight shift—say I get off at 7:00 AM, right? I drive home, the sun is coming up, traffic is starting to get heavy with all the regular nine-to-five people commuting in, and by the time I get home, take off my boots, and crawl into bed, my body is just... confused. I'll have the blackout curtains drawn tight in the bedroom, the box fan going on high for white noise because the neighborhood is just waking up and there's always delivery trucks or people mowing their lawns, but I’ll just lie there. I am so tired from being on my feet all night, but I'm just wired. It's this awful feeling where my eyes are heavy, but my mind is just buzzing and won't shut off. I'll just stare at the ceiling, tired but completely wired. \n\nAnd then, the kicker is, even when I do finally drift off, and even if I manage to stay asleep for a decent number of hours—like, say I actually get a solid seven or eight hours without the dog barking or the doorbell ringing—when I finally wake up in the late afternoon, I don't feel rested at all. I wake up feeling like I barely rested, like I just closed my eyes for five minutes. It's incredibly frustrating because you'd think after working all night you'd just pass out and wake up recharged, but my sleep is just totally broken. So no, I'm not sleeping more or taking extra daytime naps, I'm just stuck in this cycle where the sleep I do get doesn't actually do anything for me.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your appetite or weight, like eating much less than usual or losing weight without trying?\n\nPatient: No, no changes like that. My appetite is... well, it's pretty much the same as it's always been. I definitely haven't been eating less or losing any weight. If anything, I'm probably eating the exact same things I've eaten for years. \n\nYou know, when you work those weird shifts, especially the overnights, your eating schedule gets just as messed up as your sleep. So what happens is, you end up relying on whatever is open at 3:00 AM or whatever you can quickly heat up in the microwave in the breakroom. I usually pack a lunch—well, I guess you'd call it \"lunch,\" even though I eat it at like 2:30 in the morning. My wife makes these great turkey and swiss sandwiches, and she always packs a little bag of chips and an apple. And I eat it all, every single night, sitting in that quiet, echoing breakroom under those harsh fluorescent lights. I never leave anything behind. \n\nAnd then when I get home in the morning, around 8:00 AM, my wife is usually having breakfast, so I'll sit down and have a bowl of cereal or some toast with her before I try to go to sleep. And then when I wake up in the late afternoon, we have dinner together. We do a lot of chicken, rice, maybe some pasta—just regular, filling meals. And I always finish my plate. Sometimes I even go back for seconds if she makes that baked ziti I really like. \n\nSo, yeah, I'm definitely not skipping meals or losing my appetite. My clothes all fit the exact same way they did three years ago before all this schedule nonsense started. I haven't had to tighten my belt a notch or anything like that. I step on the scale in the bathroom every now and then, just out of habit, and the number hasn't budged. It's just steady. So, no, no weight loss and no eating less. My appetite is totally normal, despite everything else being so out of whack.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself feeling worthless or guilty about things more often than not, even when it doesn't seem to make sense?\n\nPatient: I mean... I don't know if 'guilty' is the exact right word, but worthless? Yeah, maybe. It’s more like this constant feeling of just... not being good enough, I guess. \n\nI look around at work, and I keep telling myself that other guys would have adjusted by now, you know? Like, the other guys who started when I did, or even after me, they get these weird schedule changes and they just roll with it. They adjust, they take on more responsibilities, they move up to shift supervisor or they transfer over to the maintenance crew where they actually get to fix the HVAC units and make better money. They figure it out. \n\nAnd then there's me. I’m still just... I'm still just trying not to miss small things at work. Like, I'll catch myself having to double-check if I actually refilled the paper towel dispensers in the second-floor bathrooms, or if I remembered to wipe down the handles on the main entrance doors. Just basic, everyday stuff that should be completely automatic by now, but I have to put all this effort into just making sure I don't screw it up. \n\nAnd because I'm struggling just to keep my head above water with the bare minimum, it really makes me feel like... well, like maybe I’m just not cut out for more than this. Like pushing that cleaning cart and emptying those bins is the absolute best I'm ever going to do in my life. My wife is always telling me I'm a hard worker, and I appreciate that, I really do, but it's hard not to feel pretty low about yourself when you see everyone else moving forward and you're just stuck, worrying about whether you forgot to empty the shredder bin in the accounting office. It just makes you feel really small, you know? Like you're just fundamentally not built for anything better.\n\nDoctor: Even though you still care about the things you used to enjoy, do you feel like you don’t get as much pleasure out of them as you used to?\n\nPatient: I don't think so, no. I mean, I still get the same kick out of things, when I actually have the time and energy to do them, anyway. Like, take fishing, for example. I've always loved fishing. There's this spot out by the reservoir, about a forty-five-minute drive from the house, right where the old county road dead-ends. It's quiet, surrounded by these big, old pine trees, and on a good day, you can pull some really decent bass out of there. I've been going there since I was in my twenties, back when I was still taking a few classes at the community college and thought I was going to get a business degree. \n\nAnyway, I still love going out there. When I'm standing on the bank, casting my line out, watching the bobber just sort of drift on the water... I still get that same feeling of peace, you know? That same little thrill when I feel a tug on the line. It's just as good as it always was. The problem isn't that I don't enjoy it anymore, it's just that with this crazy schedule—working overnight, sleeping all day, trying to squeeze in dinner with my wife before I have to start getting ready for work again—I just don't get out there as much. \n\nOr like watching baseball. I'm a huge fan, have been my whole life. I still have all my old baseball cards in binders in the hall closet. When I actually manage to sit down on the couch on my day off—if I'm not completely zombified from the shift work—and catch a game on TV, I'm still right there in it. I'm still yelling at the umpire for a bad call, still cheering when my team hits a double in the bottom of the ninth. The excitement is totally still there. I still love the crack of the bat and all the stats. I just... I might fall asleep during the seventh-inning stretch because I've been up for twenty hours, but the actual enjoyment of the game hasn't changed a bit. So, yeah, the pleasure is definitely still there for the things I like, it's just the logistics of actually doing them that's the nightmare right now.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your mood has been persistently low, feeling that heavy, flat sensation you described, for most of the day, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I would. It’s pretty much exactly like that. Most days... honestly, almost every single day, I feel like there’s this heavy, flat feeling just following me around. It’s hard to explain to people who haven't felt it, because it’s not like I’m walking around sobbing or anything dramatic like you see on TV. It’s just this... this weight. Like I’m wearing a lead vest under my uniform or something. \n\nAnd it doesn't matter where I am or what I'm doing, it's just always there. I could be at work, you know? It'll be 2:00 AM, and I'm just pushing my cleaning cart down one of those long, empty hallways on the third floor—the ones with that really ugly beige carpet that always smells faintly of industrial floor wax and stale coffee—and it's just me and the hum of the HVAC system. And that heavy feeling is right there, just pressing down on me. I'll be emptying a recycling bin or wiping down a water fountain, and it just feels like everything is muted. \n\nAnd it's not just at the building, either. That’s the part that really gets to me, because it follows me home. I’ll be sitting there at the kitchen table with my family, having dinner before I have to go sleep. My wife will be telling me about her day, maybe talking about her sister or something that happened at the grocery store, and I’m sitting there, eating my food, trying so hard to just act normal. I'll nod and smile and try to be present, but inside, I'm just totally flat. It's like there's a thick pane of glass between me and the rest of the room. So, yeah, it's there most of the day, pretty much every day. I keep waiting for it to sort of burn off, like morning fog, but it just sticks around, just this constant, heavy grayness that never really leaves me alone.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your mood has been persistently low, feeling that heavy, flat sensation you described, for most of the day, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yeah, like I was just saying, it really is nearly every day. Most days, I just feel like there’s this heavy, flat feeling following me around everywhere I go, like a shadow I can't shake. It doesn't even matter what the setting is or what I'm doing. I could be at work, just pushing my supply cart down an empty hallway at three in the morning—you know, when the building is totally dead and the only sound is the squeak of the cart's front wheel and the hum of the vending machines in the breakroom. I'll just be walking past all these locked office doors, and that weight is right there, pressing down on me. \n\nAnd it's not like it magically goes away when I clock out and go home, either. I think that's the part that exhausts me the most, honestly. I'll be sitting right there in the living room with my family, and my kids will be laughing at something on the TV, or my wife will be telling me about a phone call she had with her mom, and I'm just sitting there, trying so, so hard to just act normal. I'll force myself to smile, and I make sure to nod and say 'oh, really?' at the right times so they don't think anything is wrong, but underneath it all, I just feel completely flat and hollowed out. It takes so much energy just to put on that mask for them. So, yeah, whether I'm completely alone at work or surrounded by my family at home, that heavy feeling is just always there.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that your thoughts feel slowed down or that it's harder to think clearly, like your mind is moving through molasses, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Molasses... yeah, that's exactly it. Actually, honestly, that's a big part of why I finally came in to see someone. My head just... it just doesn’t stay on task anymore. It’s like my brain is just constantly stalling out on me. \n\nLike, at work, I'll be right in the middle of doing the monthly inventory. I have this whole system down because I've been doing it for years, right? I'll be down in the basement storage area, checking the levels on the floor wax and the industrial degreasers, and I'll realize I need to grab a specific clipboard or check a stock number from the main supply closet. The main closet is all the way on the other side of the building, past the boiler room and the old service elevators. So I'll walk all the way over there, my keys jingling, thinking about what I need to do next. But the second I unlock that door and pull the pull-string for the light, I just... I draw a complete blank. I'll just stand there staring at the shelves of paper towels and mop heads, completely forgetting what I even went to the supply closet for. I'll just stand there feeling stupid, trying to retrace my steps in my head, and it’s just empty. \n\nAnd it's really messing with my plans outside of the day-to-day cleaning, too. I've been trying to study for the supervisor certification. I took some business classes at the community college back in my twenties, so it's not like I don't know how to study or how to handle a textbook, you know? I know how to put the work in. But lately, I'll sit down at the kitchen table on my night off with the study guide—it's this thick binder with all the OSHA regulations and scheduling protocols and all that—and I just can't process any of it. I will literally sit there and keep reading the exact same line over and over and over. I'll read a sentence about, I don't know, proper ventilation requirements for chemical mixing, and my eyes will go across the words, but nothing actually enters my brain. I'll get to the end of the page and realize I haven't absorbed a single thing. It’s incredibly frustrating because I really want to pass this test and get a better shift, but it just takes me forever to get through even one paragraph now. My thoughts are just so slow and foggy.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. That same little thrill when I feel a tug on the line. It's just as good as it always was. The problem isn't that I don't enjoy it anymore, it's just that with this crazy schedule—working overnight, sleeping all day, trying to squeeze in dinner with my wife before I have to start getting ready for work again—I just don't get out there as much. \n\nOr like watching baseball. I'm a huge fan, have been my whole life. I still have all my old baseball cards in binders in the hall closet. When I actually manage to sit down on the couch on my day off—if I'm not completely zombified from the shift work—and catch a game on TV, I'm still right there in it. I'm still yelling at the umpire for a bad call, still cheering when my team hits a double in the bottom of the ninth. The excitement is totally still there. I still love the crack of the bat and all the stats. I just... I might fall asleep during the seventh-inning stretch because I've been up for twenty hours, but the actual enjoyment of the game hasn't changed a bit. So, yeah, the pleasure is definitely still there for the things I like, it's just the logistics of actually doing them that's the nightmare right now.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your mood has been persistently low, feeling that heavy, flat sensation you described, for most of the day, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I would. It’s pretty much exactly like that. Most days... honestly, almost every single day, I feel like there’s this heavy, flat feeling just following me around. It’s hard to explain to people who haven't felt it, because it’s not like I’m walking around sobbing or anything dramatic like you see on TV. It’s just this... this weight. Like I’m wearing a lead vest under my uniform or something. \n\nAnd it doesn't matter where I am or what I'm doing, it's just always there. I could be at work, you know? It'll be 2:00 AM, and I'm just pushing my cleaning cart down one of those long, empty hallways on the third floor—the ones with that really ugly beige carpet that always smells faintly of industrial floor wax and stale coffee—and it's just me and the hum of the HVAC system. And that heavy feeling is right there, just pressing down on me. I'll be emptying a recycling bin or wiping down a water fountain, and it just feels like everything is muted. \n\nAnd it's not just at the building, either. That’s the part that really gets to me, because it follows me home. I’ll be sitting there at the kitchen table with my family, having dinner before I have to go sleep. My wife will be telling me about her day, maybe talking about her sister or something that happened at the grocery store, and I’m sitting there, eating my food, trying so hard to just act normal. I'll nod and smile and try to be present, but inside, I'm just totally flat. It's like there's a thick pane of glass between me and the rest of the room. So, yeah, it's there most of the day, pretty much every day. I keep waiting for it to sort of burn off, like morning fog, but it just sticks around, just this constant, heavy grayness that never really leaves me alone.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your mood has been persistently low, feeling that heavy, flat sensation you described, for most of the day, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yeah, like I was just saying, it really is nearly every day. Most days, I just feel like there’s this heavy, flat feeling following me around everywhere I go, like a shadow I can't shake. It doesn't even matter what the setting is or what I'm doing. I could be at work, just pushing my supply cart down an empty hallway at three in the morning—you know, when the building is totally dead and the only sound is the squeak of the cart's front wheel and the hum of the vending machines in the breakroom. I'll just be walking past all these locked office doors, and that weight is right there, pressing down on me. \n\nAnd it's not like it magically goes away when I clock out and go home, either. I think that's the part that exhausts me the most, honestly. I'll be sitting right there in the living room with my family, and my kids will be laughing at something on the TV, or my wife will be telling me about a phone call she had with her mom, and I'm just sitting there, trying so, so hard to just act normal. I'll force myself to smile, and I make sure to nod and say 'oh, really?' at the right times so they don't think anything is wrong, but underneath it all, I just feel completely flat and hollowed out. It takes so much energy just to put on that mask for them. So, yeah, whether I'm completely alone at work or surrounded by my family at home, that heavy feeling is just always there.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that your thoughts feel slowed down or that it's harder to think clearly, like your mind is moving through molasses, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Molasses... yeah, that's exactly it. Actually, honestly, that's a big part of why I finally came in to see someone. My head just... it just doesn’t stay on task anymore. It’s like my brain is just constantly stalling out on me. \n\nLike, at work, I'll be right in the middle of doing the monthly inventory. I have this whole system down because I've been doing it for years, right? I'll be down in the basement storage area, checking the levels on the floor wax and the industrial degreasers, and I'll realize I need to grab a specific clipboard or check a stock number from the main supply closet. The main closet is all the way on the other side of the building, past the boiler room and the old service elevators. So I'll walk all the way over there, my keys jingling, thinking about what I need to do next. But the second I unlock that door and pull the pull-string for the light, I just... I draw a complete blank. I'll just stand there staring at the shelves of paper towels and mop heads, completely forgetting what I even went to the supply closet for. I'll just stand there feeling stupid, trying to retrace my steps in my head, and it’s just empty. \n\nAnd it's really messing with my plans outside of the day-to-day cleaning, too. I've been trying to study for the supervisor certification. I took some business classes at the community college back in my twenties, so it's not like I don't know how to study or how to handle a textbook, you know? I know how to put the work in. But lately, I'll sit down at the kitchen table on my night off with the study guide—it's this thick binder with all the OSHA regulations and scheduling protocols and all that—and I just can't process any of it. I will literally sit there and keep reading the exact same line over and over and over. I'll read a sentence about, I don't know, proper ventilation requirements for chemical mixing, and my eyes will go across the words, but nothing actually enters my brain. I'll get to the end of the page and realize I haven't absorbed a single thing. It’s incredibly frustrating because I really want to pass this test and get a better shift, but it just takes me forever to get through even one paragraph now. My thoughts are just so slow and foggy.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Yeah, doc, I mean... like I was saying a few minutes ago, that's exactly what it is. I know we sort of just touched on this, but yes, it really is most days.
